Notes

### Major camps

A rectangular logo with the text "民主進步黨" followed by "Democratic Progressive Party".

A former logo of the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party), the main [Pan-Green Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Green_Coalition) party

A circular logo representing a white sun on a blue background. The sun is a circle surrounded by twelve triangles.

Emblem of the [Kuomintang](16903.md), the main [Pan-Blue Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Blue_Coalition) party

Taiwan's political scene is divided into two major camps in terms of cross-strait relations, i.e. how Taiwan should relate to China or the PRC. The [Pan-Green Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Green_Coalition) (e.g. the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party)) leans pro-independence, and the [Pan-Blue Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Blue_Coalition) (e.g. the [Kuomintang](16903.md)) leans pro-unification.[^cite_note-335] Moderates in both camps regard the Republic of China as a sovereign independent state, but the Pan-Green Coalition regard the ROC as [synonymous with Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four-Stage_Theory_of_the_Republic_of_China),[^cite_note-336] while moderates in the Pan-Blue Coalition view it as [synonymous with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Consensus).[^cite_note-337] These positions formed against the backdrop of the PRC's [Anti-Secession Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Secession_Law), which threatens the use of "non-peaceful means" to respond to formal Taiwanese independence.[^cite_note-338] The ROC government has understood this to mean a military invasion of Taiwan.[^cite_note-339]



A [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party) event in Taipei, 2012

The [Pan-Green Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Green_Coalition) is mainly led by the pro-independence Democratic Progressive Party (DPP). They oppose the idea that Taiwan is part of China, and seek wide diplomatic recognition and an eventual declaration of formal [Taiwan independence](31156.md).[^cite_note-340] In September 2007, the ruling DPP approved a resolution asserting separate identity from China and called for the enactment of a new constitution for a "normal country". It called also for general use of "Taiwan" as the country's name, without abolishing its formal name, the "Republic of China".[^cite_note-AP2007-341] Some members of the DPP, such as former President Chen Shui-bian and President Lai Ching-te, argue that it is unnecessary to proclaim independence because Taiwan is already an independent sovereign country called the Republic of China.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343] Despite being a member of KMT prior to and during his presidency, Lee Teng-hui also held a similar view and was a supporter of the [Taiwanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanization) movement.[^cite_note-344] [Taiwan Statebuilding Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Statebuilding_Party) (TSP) and [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_\(Taiwan\)) (GPT)[^cite_note-345] have adopted a more aggressive line than the DPP, in order to win over pro-independence voters who are dissatisfied with the DPP's conservative stance.

The [Pan-Blue Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Blue_Coalition), composed of the pro-unification Kuomintang (KMT), [People First Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_First_Party_\(Republic_of_China\)) (PFP) and [New Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Party_\(Republic_of_China\)) generally support the spirit of the [1992 Consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Consensus), where the KMT claimed that there is one China, but that the ROC and PRC have different interpretations of what "China" means. They favor eventual unification with China.[^cite_note-panblue-reunif-chineseid-346] The mainstream Pan-Blue position is to maintain the status quo, while refusing immediate unification.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] Former President Ma Ying-jeou defined cross-strait relations as special relations, not one between two Chinas or two states.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-chinapost.com.tw-350][^cite_note-MasClaimtoMainland-351] Ma also noted that unification is unlikely within our lifetimes, as the Taiwanese people oppose the mainland's authoritarian rule.[^cite_note-352] Some Pan-Blue members seek to improve relationships with the PRC, with a focus on improving economic ties.[^cite_note-353]

### National identity

Main article: [Taiwanese people § The current state of Taiwanese identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_people#The_current_state_of_Taiwanese_identity)

See also: [Taiwan independence movement](31156.md) and [Chinese unification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_unification)

Roughly 84 percent of Taiwan's population are descendants of [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese) immigrants between 1683 and 1895. Another significant fraction descends from Han Chinese who immigrated from mainland China in the late 1940s and early 1950s. The shared cultural origin as well as hostility between the rival ROC and PRC have resulted in national identity being a contentious issue with political overtones.

Since democratic reforms and the lifting of [martial law](50477.md), a distinct Taiwanese identity is often at the heart of political debates. Its acceptance makes the island distinct from mainland China, and therefore may be seen as a step towards forming a consensus for *de jure* Taiwan independence.[^cite_note-power-shift-354] The [Pan-Green camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Green_camp) supports a predominantly Taiwanese identity (although "Chinese" may be viewed as cultural heritage), while the [Pan-Blue camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-blue_camp) supports a predominantly Chinese identity (with "Taiwanese" as a regional/diasporic Chinese identity).[^cite_note-panblue-reunif-chineseid-346] The KMT has downplayed this stance in the recent years and now supports a Taiwanese identity as part of a Chinese identity.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356]

#### Public opinion

See also: [Opinion polling on Taiwanese identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_Taiwanese_identity)



Results from an identity survey conducted each year from 1992 to 2020 by the Election Study Center, [National Chengchi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Chengchi_University).

[^cite_note-nccu-357]

&#x20;Responses are Taiwanese (green), Chinese (red) or Both Taiwanese and Chinese (hatched). No response is shown as gray.

Taiwanese identification has increased substantially since the early 1990s, while Chinese identification has fallen to a low level, and identification as both has also seen a reduction. In 1992, 17.6 percent of respondents identified as Taiwanese, 25.5 percent as Chinese, 46.4 percent as both, and 10.5 percent non-response. In June 2021, 63.3 percent identified as Taiwanese, 2.6 percent as Chinese, 31.4 percent as both, and 2.7 percent non-response.[^cite_note-nccu-357] A survey conducted in Taiwan by Global Views Survey Research Center in July 2009 showed that 82.8 percent of respondents consider the ROC and the PRC two separate countries with each developing on its own but 80.2 percent think they are members of the Chinese.[^cite_note-358]

Domestic public opinion has preferred maintaining the status quo, though pro-independence sentiment has steadily risen since 1994. In June 2021, an annual poll found that 28.2 percent supported the status quo and postponing a decision, 27.5 percent supported maintaining the status quo indefinitely, 25.8 percent supported the status quo with a move toward independence, 5.9 percent supported the status quo with a move toward unification, 5.7 percent gave no response, 5.6 percent supported independence as soon as possible, and 1.5 percent supported unification as soon as possible.[^cite_note-chengchiIndepUnif-359] A [referendum question in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Taiwanese_referendum) asked if Taiwan's athletes should compete under "Taiwan" in the [2020 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics) but did not pass; the *New York Times* attributed the failure to a campaign cautioning that a name change might lead to Taiwan being banned "under Chinese pressure".[^cite_note-HortonNYT2018-360]

### International status and relations

Main articles: [Foreign relations of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Taiwan), [Political status of Taiwan](66555.md), and [International status of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_status_of_Taiwan)

See also: [List of states with limited recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_with_limited_recognition); [Foreign relations of China § International territorial disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_China#International_territorial_disputes); and [Taiwan, China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan,_China)



&#x20; Republic of China (Taiwan)

&#x20; Countries that have formal relations with Taiwan

&#x20; Countries that have formal relations with the PRC and informal relations with Taiwan

The political and legal statuses of Taiwan are contentious issues. The People's Republic of China (PRC) claims that Taiwan is Chinese territory and that the PRC became the successor state of the ROC in 1949, thence being the sole legal government of China.[^cite_note-PRCNorway-100] The ROC, however, has its own armed forces, constitution, institutions, territory under control, [currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Taiwan_dollar), [widely accepted passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_passport), [postage stamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postage_stamps_and_postal_history_of_Taiwan), and internet [TLD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top-level_domain), etc.[^cite_note-tfp09-361] It has not formally renounced its claim to the mainland, but ROC government publications have increasingly downplayed the matter.[^cite_note-362]

Until 1928, the [foreign policy](233404.md) of Republican China was complicated by a lack of internal unity—competing centers of power all claimed legitimacy. This situation changed after the defeat of the [Peiyang Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beiyang_Government) by the Kuomintang (KMT), which led to widespread diplomatic recognition of the Republic of China.[^cite_note-363] After the KMT retreated to Taiwan, most countries, especially those of the [Western Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) – save the United Kingdom, which recognized the PRC in 1950[^cite_note-364] – continued to maintain formal relations with the ROC; but recognition gradually eroded and many countries switched recognition to the People's Republic of China in the 1970s. On 25 October 1971, UN Resolution 2758 was adopted by 76 votes to 35 with 17 abstentions, recognizing the PRC as China's sole representative in the United Nations.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366]



[ROC embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_Republic_of_China_\(Taiwan\)_in_the_Kingdom_of_Eswatini) in [Eswatini](27451.md)

The PRC refuses to have [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_China) with any nation that has diplomatic relations with the ROC, and requires all nations with which it has diplomatic relations to make a statement on its claims to Taiwan.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368] As a result, only [11 UN member states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_the_Republic_of_China) and the [Holy See](13393.md) maintain official diplomatic relations with the Republic of China.[^cite_note-ap2024-369] The ROC maintains unofficial relations with other countries via *de facto* [embassies](8970.md) and [consulates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consul_\(representative\)) mostly called [Taipei Economic and Cultural Representative Offices](745357.md) (TECRO), with branch offices called "Taipei Economic and Cultural Offices" (TECO). Both TECRO and TECO are "unofficial commercial entities" of the ROC in charge of maintaining diplomatic relations, providing consular services, and serving the national interests of the ROC.[^cite_note-370]

From 1954 to 1979, the United States had a [mutual defense treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_Defense_Treaty_between_the_United_States_and_the_Republic_of_China) with the ROC. Even after [establishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Communiqu%C3%A9_on_the_Establishment_of_Diplomatic_Relations) official relations with the PRC, the US remains one of the [main supporters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China-United_States_relations) of Taiwan and, through the [Taiwan Relations Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Relations_Act), has continued selling arms and providing military training to the [Republic of China Armed Forces](30095.md). The PRC considers US involvement as an interference to its internal affairs and claims it to be disruptive to the stability of the region.[^cite_note-SanctionsAFP-371][^cite_note-372] The official position of the United States is that it "does not support any unilateral change to the status quo in the Taiwan Strait".[^cite_note-373] While not officially classified as a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md), it has been *de facto* treated as such by the [United States](3434750.md) since at least 2003.[^cite_note-Kan20092-374]

Taiwan, since 2016 under the Tsai administration's [New Southbound Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Southbound_Policy), has pursued closer economic relations with South and [Southeast Asian](28741.md) countries, increasing cooperation on investments and people-to-people exchanges despite the region's general lack of official diplomatic ties with Taipei.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] The policy has led to Taiwan receiving an increased number of migrants and students from the region.[^cite_note-377] However, a few scandals involving Southeast Asians, particularly Indonesians, experiencing exploitation in scholarship programs[^cite_note-378] and certain industries have emerged as setbacks for the policy[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] as well as for [Indonesia-Taiwan relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia-Taiwan_relations).[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382]

#### Relations with the PRC

Main article: [Cross-strait relations](2596399.md)



The [2015 Ma–Xi meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Ma%E2%80%93Xi_meeting) was the only meeting between the leaders of both sides of the Taiwan Strait since 1949.

On 30 April 1991, President Lee Teng-hui announced the repeal of the [Temporary Provisions against the Communist Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_Provisions_against_the_Communist_Rebellion), characterizing the Chinese Communist Party as "a political entity that controls the mainland region" or the "mainland authorities" instead and declaring that "we will no longer seek to unify China through force."[^cite_note-383]

The [Mainland Affairs Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Affairs_Council) (MAC) of Taiwan is responsible for relations with the PRC, while the [Taiwan Affairs Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Affairs_Office) (TAO) of the PRC is responsible for relations with Taiwan. Exchanges are conducted through private organizations both founded in 1991: the [Straits Exchange Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_Exchange_Foundation) (SEF) of Taiwan and the [Association for Relations Across the Taiwan Straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Relations_Across_the_Taiwan_Straits) (ARATS) of the PRC.

In November 1992, the ARATS and SEF held a meeting which would later become known as the [1992 Consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Consensus). The SEF announced that both sides agreed that there was only [one China](66739.md), but disagreed on the definition of China, i.e. ROC and PRC. Meanwhile, the ARATS announced that the two sides agreed on the One China principle, but did not mention the differences regarding its definition as made in the SEF statement.[^cite_note-384] In Taiwan, the ruling [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party) has rejected the 1992 Consensus since early 2019.[^cite_note-385] President [Lai Ching-te](22609914.md) has stated that the ROC and the PRC are not subordinate to each other.[^cite_note-386]

The PRC's One China principle states that Taiwan and mainland China are both part of China, and that the PRC is the only legitimate government of China.[^cite_note-woo21-387] It seeks to prevent or reduce any formal recognition of the ROC as an independent sovereign state,[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] meaning that Taiwan participates in many international forums as a non-state member under names such as "Chinese Taipei". The PRC has suggested the "[one country, two systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems)" concept employed in [Hong Kong](13404.md) as a model for a peaceful unification with Taiwan.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391] While it aims for peaceful reunification, the PRC does not rule out the use of force.[^cite_note-scmp2014sep27-392][^cite_note-bbc21-393] The political environment is complicated by the potential for military conflict[^cite_note-mol21-394][^cite_note-mar21-395][^cite_note-yn21-396] should events outlined in the PRC's [Anti-Secession Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Secession_Law) occur, such as Taiwan declaring *[de jure](43594.md)* independence. There is a substantial military presence on the Fujian coast, sorties into Taiwan's [air defense identification zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_defense_identification_zone) (ADIZ), as well as increasing military drills in recent years.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-2004NatDefRpt-398][^cite_note-srf21-399]

#### Participation in international events and organizations

See also: [Foreign relations of Taiwan § Relation with International organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Taiwan#Relation_with_International_organizations), and [Chinese Taipei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Taipei)

The ROC was a [founding member of the United Nations](31969.md#Original_members), and held the [seat of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_the_United_Nations) on the [Security Council](31956.md) and other UN bodies until 1971, when it was expelled by Resolution 2758 and replaced with the PRC. The ROC now has neither official membership nor observer status in the organization. Since 1993, the ROC has petitioned the UN for entry, but its applications have not made it past committee stage.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401] Due to the [One China](66739.md) principle, most [UN member states](31969.md), including the United States, do not wish to discuss the issue of the ROC's political status for fear of souring diplomatic relations with the PRC.[^cite_note-402]

A white symbol in shape of a five petal flower ringed by a blue and a red line. In its center stands a circular symbol depicting a white sun on a blue background. The five Olympic circles (blue, yellow, black, green and red) stand below it.

The flag used by Taiwan at the Olympic Games, where it competes as "[Chinese Taipei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Taipei)" (中華台北)

[National Flag Anthem of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Flag_Anthem_of_the_Republic_of_China) used by Taiwan at the Olympic Games

&#x20;At one point, the ROC government shifted its focus to organizations affiliated with the UN, as well as organizations outside the UN system.[^cite_note-403] The government sought to participate in the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) since 1997;[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] efforts were rejected until 2009, when they participated as an observer under the name "Chinese Taipei" after reaching an agreement with Beijing.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] In 2017, Taiwan again came to be excluded from the WHO even in an observer capacity.[^cite_note-408] This exclusion was instrumental to a number of scandals during the [COVID-19](63030231.md) outbreak.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410]

The [Nagoya Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_at_the_Olympics#Nagoya_Resolution) in 1979 approved by the [International Olympic Committee](15147.md) (IOC) provided a compromise for the ROC to use the name "Chinese Taipei" in international events, such as the [Olympic Games](22576.md).[^cite_note-Brownell2007-411][^cite_note-Lin-412][^cite_note-chinese-taipei-413] Under the IOC charter, [ROC flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_Republic_of_China) cannot be flown at any official Olympic venue or gathering.[^cite_note-414] The ROC also participates in the [Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation) forum (since 1991) and the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (since 2002) under the names "Chinese Taipei" and "Separate Customs Territory of Taiwan, Penghu, Kinmen and Matsu", respectively.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416] It was a founding member of the [Asian Development Bank](2512.md), but since China's ascension in 1986 has participated under the name "Taipei, China". The ROC is able to participate as "China" in organizations in which the PRC does not participate, such as the [World Organization of the Scout Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Organization_of_the_Scout_Movement).

Due to its limited international recognition, the Republic of China has been a member of the [Unrepresented Nations and Peoples Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrepresented_Nations_and_Peoples_Organization) (UNPO) since the foundation of the organization in 1991, represented by a government-funded organization, the [Taiwan Foundation for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Foundation_for_Democracy) (TFD), under the name "Taiwan".[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418]

### Military

Main article: [Republic of China Armed Forces](30095.md)

See also: [Military history of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Taiwan) and [Republic of China Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_Military_Academy)



A Taiwanese [F-16](11642.md) fighter jet flies next to a Chinese [H-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xian_H-6) bomber (top) in Taiwan's [ADIZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_defense_identification_zone).

The [Republic of China Armed Forces](30095.md) takes its roots in the [National Revolutionary Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Revolutionary_Army), which was established by [Sun Yat-sen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Yat-sen) in 1924 in [Guangdong](65109.md) with a goal of reunifying China under the Kuomintang. When the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) won the Chinese Civil War, much of the National Revolutionary Army retreated to Taiwan along with the government. The 1947 Constitution of the ROC reformed it into the Republic of China Armed Forces, making it the national army rather than the army of a political party. Units which surrendered and remained in mainland China were either disbanded or incorporated into the People's Liberation Army.

From 1949 to the 1970s, the primary mission of the Taiwanese military was to "retake mainland China" through Project National Glory. As this mission has transitioned away from attack because the relative strength of the PRC has massively increased, the ROC military has begun to shift emphasis from the traditionally dominant Army to the [air force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_Air_Force) and [navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_Navy). Control of the armed forces has also passed into the hands of the civilian government.[^cite_note-towards-419][^cite_note-420]

The ROC began a series of force reduction plans since the 1990s to scale down its military from a level of 450,000 in 1997 to 380,000 in 2001.[^cite_note-421] As of 2021, the total strength of the Armed Forces is capped at 215,000 with 90 percent manning ratio for volunteer military.[^cite_note-422] [Conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Taiwan) remains universal for qualified males reaching age eighteen, but as a part of the reduction effort many are given the opportunity to fulfill their draft requirement through alternative service.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424] The military's reservists is around 2.5 million including first-wave reservists numbered at 300,000 as of 2022.[^cite_note-425] Taiwan's [defense spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget) as a percentage of its GDP fell below three percent in 1999 and had been trending downwards over the first two decades of the twenty-first century.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] The ROC government pledged to raise the spending as high as proposed three percent of GDP.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] In 2024, Taiwan proposed 2.45 percent of projected GDP in defense spending for the following year.[^cite_note-431]



The [Han Kuang Exercise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Kuang_Exercise) is an annual military exercise by the [ROC Armed Forces](30095.md) in preparation for a possible attack from the PRC.

The ROC and the United States signed the Sino-American Mutual Defense Treaty in 1954, and established the [United States Taiwan Defense Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Taiwan_Defense_Command). About 30,000 US troops were stationed in Taiwan, until the United States established diplomatic relations with the PRC in 1979.[^cite_note-432] A significant amount of military hardware has been bought from the United States, and continues to be legally guaranteed by the [Taiwan Relations Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Relations_Act). France and the Netherlands have also sold military weapons and hardware to the ROC, but they almost entirely stopped in the 1990s under pressure of the PRC.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

There is no guarantee in the Taiwan Relations Act or any other treaty that the United States will defend Taiwan, even in the event of invasion.[^cite_note-435] The joint declaration on security between the US and Japan signed in 1996 may imply that Japan would be involved in any response. However, Japan has refused to stipulate whether the "area surrounding Japan" mentioned in the pact includes Taiwan.[^cite_note-436] The [Australia, New Zealand, United States Security Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia,_New_Zealand,_United_States_Security_Treaty) (ANZUS Treaty) may mean that other US allies, such as Australia, could be involved.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-sei21-438] While this would risk damaging economic ties with China,[^cite_note-439] a conflict over Taiwan could lead to an economic blockade of China by a greater coalition.[^cite_note-440][^cite_note-chinas_fear_2019_04_30_reuters-441][^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444]

### LGBTQ rights

Main articles: [LGBTQ rights in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Taiwan) and [Same-sex marriage in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Taiwan)

On 24 May 2017, the [Constitutional Court](378790.md#Constitutional_Court) ruled that then-current marriage laws had been violating the Constitution by denying same-sex couples the right to marry. The Court ruled that if the Legislative Yuan did not pass adequate amendments to Taiwanese marriage laws within two years, same-sex marriages would automatically become lawful in Taiwan.[^cite_note-445] In a [referendum question in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Taiwanese_referendum), however, voters expressed overwhelming opposition to same-sex marriage and supported the removal of content about homosexuality from primary school textbooks. According to the *New York Times*, the referendum questions were subject to a "well-funded and highly organized campaign led by conservative Christians and other groups" involving the use of misinformation.[^cite_note-HortonNYT2018-360] Nevertheless, the vote against same-sex marriage does not affect the court ruling, and on 17 May 2019, Taiwan's parliament approved a bill legalizing same-sex marriage, making it the first country in Asia to do so.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448]

Taiwan has an annual pride event, [Taiwan Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Pride). It currently holds the record for the largest [LGBTQ](78174325.md) gathering in [East Asia](19605700.md), rivaling [Tel Aviv Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_Pride) in [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-449] The event draws more than 200,000 people.[^cite_note-450]

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Taiwan](30092.md) and [Economic history of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Taiwan)

Photo of Taipei 101 tower against a blue sky.

[Taipei 101](203801.md) held the record for the world's tallest skyscraper from 2004 to 2009.

The quick industrialization and rapid growth of Taiwan during the latter half of the 20th century has been called the "[Taiwan Miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Miracle)". Taiwan is one of the "[Four Asian Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Asian_Tigers)" alongside Hong Kong, South Korea and Singapore. As of 2025, Taiwan is the 22nd largest [economy in the world by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)).[^cite_note-IMF2025-451]

Since 2001, agriculture has constituted less than 2 percent of GDP, down from 32 percent in 1951.[^cite_note-452] Unlike its neighbors, South Korea and Japan, the Taiwanese economy is dominated by [small and medium-sized enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium-sized_enterprises), rather than the large business groups.[^cite_note-453] Traditional labor-intensive industries are steadily being moved offshore, replaced by more capital- and technology-intensive industries. [High-technology science parks](30092.md#Science_and_industrial_parks) have sprung up across Taiwan.

Today Taiwan has a dynamic, [capitalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism), export-driven economy. Exports provide the primary impetus for industrialization. The ratio of Taiwan's exports to GDP is 76%.[^cite_note-\:19-454] The trade surplus is substantial, and Taiwan remains one of the [world's largest forex reserve holders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves).[^cite_note-455] Taiwan's total trade in 2025 reached US\$1.12 trillion. Both exports and imports for the year reached record levels, totaling US\$640.75 billion and US\$479.4 billion, respectively.[^cite_note-456] China and the United States are Taiwan's two largest trading partners, together accounting for over 40 percent of total trade.[^cite_note-BoFT-457] Taiwan's economy relies heavily on [semiconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor) and other AI-related gear exports, which account for around 80% of its total exports. Consumption, by contrast, plays a relatively small role, accounting for 40% of Taiwan's GDP.[^cite_note-\:19-454]

Since the beginning of the 1990s, economic ties between Taiwan and China have been extensive. In 2002, China surpassed the United States to become Taiwan's largest export market for the first time.[^cite_note-458] China was the top destination of Taiwanese investment from 1990 to 2020.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460] This has shifted dramatically toward Southeast Asia due to rising labor costs and the effects of the [China–United States trade war](57055272.md), as well as toward the U.S., Japan, and Korea due to the overseas operations of [Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMC)](463574.md).[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462] TSMC's expansions have helped increase the proportion of Taiwanese employed in the U.S., although China still hosts the largest share.[^cite_note-463]



[TSMC](463574.md) fab 5 located in [Hsinchu Science Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hsinchu_Science_Park)

Since the 1980s, a number of Taiwan-based technology firms have expanded their reach around the world.[^cite_note-464] Taiwan is a key player in the supply chain for advanced [chips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_circuit). Taiwan's rise in the key semiconductor industry was largely attributed to Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMC) and [United Microelectronic Corporation (UMC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Microelectronics_Corporation).[^cite_note-465] TSMC was founded 21 February 1987 and as of December 2021 its [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) equated to roughly 90% of Taiwan's GDP.[^cite_note-466] The company is the 9th largest in the world by market capitalization[^cite_note-467] as well as the world's biggest semiconductor manufacturing company, surpassing [Intel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intel) and [Samsung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung_Semiconductor_Inc.).[^cite_note-468] UMC, another major company in Taiwan's high-tech exports and global semiconductors, competes with the American [GlobalFoundries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GlobalFoundries), and others, for less advanced semiconductor processes and for silicon wafers.[^cite_note-469] Other well-known international technology companies based in Taiwan include personal computer manufacturers [Acer Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acer_Inc.) and [Asus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asus), as well as [Foxconn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foxconn), an electronics contract manufacturer.[^cite_note-470]

Taiwan is a major manufacturer of bicycles and their component parts and accounts for around 40% of all bikes imported to Europe. French newspaper *[Le Monde diplomatique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde_diplomatique)* alleged that in the past several decades, the industry has relied on exploitative [migrant labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migrant_worker) practices to boost its productivity. Workers recruited from poorer neighboring countries face [debt bondage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt_bondage), passport retention, wage withholding and substandard living conditions, as well as threats and intimidation from employers. Taiwan is one of the few countries where labor brokers are permitted to charge high recruitment fees to migrant workers. Recent reporting on these practices has led to promises of reform from some manufacturers.[^cite_note-471] In response to the allegations, the Taiwanese trade association stated that all bicycle manufacturers complied with the labor laws and proactively worked to improve working conditions.[^cite_note-472]

### Transport

Main article: [Transportation in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Taiwan)



[China Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Airlines) aircraft lineup at [Taoyuan International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoyuan_International_Airport)

The [Ministry of Transportation and Communications](17457361.md) of Taiwan is the cabinet-level governing body of the transport network in Taiwan. Civilian transport in Taiwan is characterized by extensive use of [scooters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scooters_in_Taiwan?action=edit\&redlink=1). In March 2019, 13.86 million were registered, twice that of cars.[^cite_note-473] Both highways and railways are concentrated near the coasts, where the majority of the population resides, with 1,619 km (1,006 mi) of [motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway). Railways in Taiwan are primarily used for passenger services, with [Taiwan Railways Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Railways_Administration) (TRA) operating a circular route around the island and [Taiwan High Speed Rail (THSR)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_High_Speed_Rail) running high speed services on the west coast. Urban transit systems include [Taipei Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taipei_Metro), [Kaohsiung Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaohsiung_Metro), [Taoyuan Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoyuan_Metro), [New Taipei Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Taipei_Metro), and [Taichung MRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taichung_MRT).

Major airports include [Taiwan Taoyuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Taoyuan_International_Airport), [Kaohsiung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaohsiung_International_Airport), [Taipei Songshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taipei_Songshan_Airport) and [Taichung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taichung_Airport). There are currently seven Taiwanese passenger airlines, with the largest two being [China Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Airlines) and [EVA Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EVA_Air). There are seven international seaports: [Keelung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Keelung), [Taipei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Taipei), Suao, [Taichung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Taichung), [Kaohsiung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Kaohsiung), [Anping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Anping), and [Hualien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Hualien).[^cite_note-474] The Port of Kaohsiung handled the largest volume of cargo in Taiwan, with about 440 million shipping tonnes, which accounted for 58.6% of Taiwan's total throughput in 2021.[^cite_note-475] The shipping tonnage followed by Taichung (18.6%), Taipei (12%) and Keelung (8.7%).

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Taiwan)



Population density map of Taiwan (residents per square kilometer)

Taiwan has a population of about 23.3 million,[^cite_note-population-28] most of whom are on the island of Taiwan. The remainder live on the outlying islands of Penghu, Kinmen, and Matsu.[^cite_note-taiwan-popstat-476] It has one of the world's lowest fertility rates, with the average number of children born per woman falling below 0.8, far beneath the replacement level of 2.1 needed to maintain a stable population.[^cite_note-477] Under United Nations criteria, Taiwan has become a super-aged society, with more than 20% of its population aged 65 or older. The aging population has raised concerns about the long-term sustainability of Taiwan's National Health Insurance system, as a shrinking workforce supports a growing elderly population.[^cite_note-478] Declining birth rates have also led to falling school enrollments, resulting in the merger or closure of some rural elementary and middle schools, with similar pressures increasingly affecting universities. Labor shortages have emerged across multiple sectors, including manufacturing, construction, agriculture, and elderly care.[^cite_note-479]

### Ethnic groups

Main articles: [Taiwanese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_people), [Han Taiwanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Taiwanese), [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese), [Taiwanese indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_indigenous_peoples), and [Plains indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_indigenous_peoples)

The ROC government reports that 95 percent of the population is ethnically [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese).[^cite_note-480] There are also 2.5 percent indigenous [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) peoples and 2.5 percent new immigrants primarily from China and Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-mofa2024-20]

Most [Han Taiwanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Taiwanese) are descended from the [Hoklo people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoklo_people), native to the coastal regions of southern [Fujian](88585.md), and the [Hakka people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people), native to eastern [Guangdong](65109.md). Hoklo and Hakka migrants arrived in large numbers during the 17th and 18th century. Descendants of Hoklo now compose approximately 70 percent of Taiwan's population.[^cite_note-cia-factbook-23] Descendants of Hakka make up about 15 percent of the population. Another minority group, called *[waishengren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waishengren)*, comprises those who arrived from China during the 1940s or are descended from them.[^cite_note-481] [Genetic studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians) indicate that the Han people in Taiwan are closer to southern Chinese compared to northern Chinese and, similar to several other groups of Han Chinese, had mixed with Island [Southeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Southeast_Asia), likely before their migration to Taiwan.[^cite_note-482] Nonetheless, they can be genetically distinguished from the indigenous minority despite low-level admixture. In addition, there is also evidence of genetic influence from groups like [western Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Indonesia) and [Mainland Southeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia), especially [Kinh Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinh_Vietnamese).[^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484]

[Taiwanese Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Indigenous_peoples) number about 584,000, and the government recognizes 16 groups.[^cite_note-485] The [Ami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amis_people), [Atayal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atayal_people), [Bunun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunun_people), [Kanakanavu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanakanavu_people), [Kavalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kavalan_people), [Paiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paiwan_people), [Puyuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puyuma_people), [Rukai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukai_people), [Saisiyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saisiyat_people), [Saaroa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saaroa_people), [Sakizaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakizaya_people), [Sediq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sediq_people), [Thao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thao_people), [Truku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truku_people) and [Tsou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsou_people) live mostly in the eastern half of the island, while the [Yami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yami_people) inhabit [Orchid Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchid_Island).[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487] Taiwan's Indigenous peoples experience significantly lower life expectancy than the Han Chinese population.[^cite_note-488]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Taiwan)



Most commonly used home language in each area, darker in proportion to the lead over the next most common:

&#x20; [Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese)

&#x20; [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien) or Min Nan

&#x20; [Hakka Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese)

&#x20; [Austronesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages)

The Republic of China does not have any legally designated [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language). [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Mandarin) is the primary language used in business and education, and is spoken by the vast majority of the population. [Traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese) is used as the writing system.[^cite_note-yb-languages-489]

Around 70% of Taiwan's population belong to the [Hoklo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoklo) ethnic group and are native speakers of [Taiwanese Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Hokkien).[^cite_note-490] The [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka) group, comprising some 14–18 percent of the population, speak [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese). Although Mandarin is the language of instruction in schools and dominates television and radio, non-Mandarin [Chinese varieties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Chinese) have undergone a revival in public life in Taiwan, particularly since restrictions on their use were lifted in the 1990s.[^cite_note-yb-languages-489]

[Formosan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formosan_languages) are spoken primarily by the indigenous peoples of Taiwan. They do not belong to the Chinese or Sino-Tibetan language family, but to the [Austronesian language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages), and are written in the [Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_systems_of_Formosan_languages).[^cite_note-491] Their use among aboriginal minority groups has been in decline as usage of Mandarin has risen.[^cite_note-yb-languages-489] Of the 14 extant languages, five are considered [moribund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moribund_language).[^cite_note-492]

Since the [May Fourth Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Fourth_Movement), [written vernacular Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Written_vernacular_Chinese) had replaced [Classical Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Chinese) and emerged as the mainstream written Chinese in the Republic of China. Classical Chinese continued to be widely used in government documents until reforms in the 1970s to shift the written style to a more integrated vernacular Chinese and Classical Chinese style (文白合一行文).[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494] On 1 January 2005, the [Executive Yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Yuan) also changed its long-standing convention on the [direction of writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizontal_and_vertical_writing_in_East_Asian_scripts) in official documents from vertical to horizontal. Standalone Classical Chinese is occasionally used in formal or ceremonial occasions, such as religious or cultural rites. The "[National Anthem of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anthem_of_the_Republic_of_China)" (中華民國國歌), for example, is in Classical Chinese. Most official [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Republic_of_China), [legal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Taiwan), and [judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(Taiwan\)) documents, as well as [courts rulings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_Republic_of_China) use a combined vernacular Chinese and Classical Chinese style.[^cite_note-495] As many legal documents are still written in Classical Chinese, which is not easily understood by the general public, a group of Taiwanese have launched the Legal Vernacular Movement, hoping to bring more vernacular Chinese into the legal writings of the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China).[^cite_note-496]

Taiwan is officially multilingual. A national language in Taiwan is legally defined as "a natural language used by an original people group of Taiwan and the [Taiwan Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Sign_Language)".[^cite_note-natLangAct-14]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Taiwan)



Main altar of the Shrine of [Koxinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koxinga) in Tainan.

The Constitution of the Republic of China protects people's [freedom of religion](46006.md) and the practices of belief.[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498] [The government respects freedom of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_Taiwan), and Taiwan scores highly on the [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy Indices for religious freedom.[^cite_note-499]

In 2005, the census reported that the five largest religious groups were [Buddhism](3267529.md), [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism), [Yiguandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiguandao), [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism), and [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism).[^cite_note-religion-500] According to [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), the religious composition of Taiwan in 2023[^cite_note-Pew_religion_stats-22] was 28 percent [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Taiwan), 24% Taoist, 7% Christian, 5% combination of religions, 4% local/indigenous, 27% no religion, and 4% others. Taiwanese aborigines comprise a notable subgroup among professing Christians.[^cite_note-501] There has been [a small Muslim community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Taiwan) of [Hui people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people) since the 17th century.[^cite_note-502]

[Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism) serves as the foundation of both Chinese and Taiwanese culture. The majority of Taiwanese people usually combine the secular moral teachings of Confucianism with whatever religions they are affiliated with.

As of 2019, there were 15,175 [religious buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_of_worship) in Taiwan, approximately one place of worship per 1,572 residents. 12,279 temples were dedicated to Taoism and Buddhism. There were 9,684 Taoist temples, 2,317 Buddhist temples,[^cite_note-表23各宗教教務概況-503] and 2,845 Christian churches.[^cite_note-表23各宗教教務概況-503] On average, there is one temple, church, or religious building for every square kilometer. The density of religions and religious buildings in Taiwan is among the highest in the world.[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]

A significant percentage of the population is non-religious. Taiwan's lack of state-sanctioned discrimination, and generally high regard for freedom of religion or belief earned it a joint #1 ranking in the 2018 [Freedom of Thought Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_Thought_Report).[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507] On the other hand, the [Indonesian migrant worker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_migrant_worker) community in Taiwan (estimated to total 258,084 people) has experienced religious restrictions by local employers or the government.[^cite_note-508][^cite_note-509]

### Education

Main articles: [Education in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Taiwan), [Academia Sinica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Sinica), and [History of education in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_education_in_Taiwan)

See also: [Scholarships in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholarships_in_Taiwan) and [Economy of Taiwan § Science](30092.md#Science)



The gate of [National Taiwan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Taiwan_University), which is widely considered to be the most prestigious university in Taiwan

[^cite_note-510]

Taiwan is well known for adhering to the Confucian paradigm of valuing education as a means to improve one's socioeconomic position in society.[^cite_note-511][^cite_note-The_Australian-2012-512] Heavy investment and a cultural valuing of education has made the resource-poor nation to be consistently ranked to the top of global education rankings. Taiwan is one of the top-performing countries in reading literacy, mathematics and sciences. In 2015, Taiwanese students achieved one of the world's best results in mathematics, science and literacy, as tested by the [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) (PISA), with the average student scoring 519, compared with the OECD average of 493, placing it seventh in the world.[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515]

The Taiwanese education system has been praised for its comparatively high test results and its major role in promoting Taiwan's economic development while creating one of the world's most highly educated workforces.[^cite_note-516][^cite_note-517] Taiwan has also been praised for its high university entrance rate where the university acceptance rate has increased from around 20 percent before the 1980s to 49 percent in 1996 and over 95 percent since 2008, among the highest in Asia.[^cite_note-Chou-2014-518][^cite_note-519][^cite_note-Hsueh-2018-520] The nation's high university entrance rate has created a highly skilled workforce making Taiwan one of the most highly educated countries in the world with 68.5 percent of Taiwanese high school students going on to attend university.[^cite_note-Sechiyama-2013-521] Taiwan has a high percentage of its citizens holding a tertiary education degree where 45 percent of Taiwanese aged 25–64 hold a bachelor's degree or higher compared with the average of 33 percent among member countries of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD).[^cite_note-Hsueh-2018-520][^cite_note-522]

On the other hand, the education system has been criticized for placing excessive pressure on students while eschewing creativity and producing an excess supply of overeducated university graduates. Many graduates consequently face unemployment or [underemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underemployment) due to a lack of graduate-level jobs.[^cite_note-523][^cite_note-The_Australian-2012-512] Taiwan's universities have also been under criticism for not being able to fully meet the requirements and demands of Taiwan's 21st-century fast-moving labor market, citing a skills mismatch among a large number of self-assessed, overeducated graduates who do not fit the demands of the modern Taiwanese labor market.[^cite_note-524] The Taiwanese government has been criticized for failing to adequately address this discrepancy in labor supply and demand.[^cite_note-Chou-2014-518][^cite_note-525]

As the Taiwanese economy is largely science and technology based, the labor market demands people who have achieved some form of higher education, particularly related to science and engineering to gain a competitive edge when searching for employment. Although current Taiwanese law mandates only nine years of schooling, 95 percent of junior high graduates go on to attend a senior vocational high school, university, junior college, trade school, or other higher education institution.[^cite_note-Sechiyama-2013-521][^cite_note-526] Many Taiwanese students attend [cram schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cram_schools#Taiwan), or *buxiban*, to improve skills and knowledge on problem solving against exams.[^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528]

Since [Made in China 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Made_in_China_2025) was announced in 2015, aggressive campaigns to recruit Taiwanese chip industry talent to support its mandates resulted in the loss of more than 3,000 chip engineers to mainland China,[^cite_note-Nikkei-529] and raised concerns of a "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain)" in Taiwan.[^cite_note-530][^cite_note-531]

As of 2020, the [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_rate) in Taiwan was 99.03 percent.[^cite_note-532]

### Health

Main article: [Healthcare in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Taiwan)



[National Taiwan University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Taiwan_University_Hospital)

The current [healthcare system](60976.md), known as National Health Insurance (NHI), was instituted in 1995. NHI is a [single-payer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care) compulsory social insurance plan that centralizes the disbursement of healthcare funds. The system promises equal access to healthcare for all citizens, and the population coverage had reached 99 percent by the end of 2004.[^cite_note-533] NHI is mainly financed through premiums, which are based on the payroll tax, and is supplemented with out-of-pocket co-payments and direct government funding.[^cite_note-534][^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536] Low-income families, veterans, centenarians, children under three, and catastrophic diseases are exempt from co-payments. Co-pays are reduced for disabled and low-income households maintain 100 percent premium coverage.

Early in the program, the payment system was predominantly [fee-for-service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fee-for-service). Most health providers operate in the private sector and form a competitive market on the health delivery side. However, many healthcare providers took advantage of the system by offering unnecessary services. In the face of increasing loss and the need for cost containment, NHI changed the payment system from fee-for-service to a global budget, a kind of [prospective payment system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospective_payment_system), in 2002.

The implementation of universal healthcare created fewer [health disparities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_disparities) for lower-income citizens in Taiwan. According to a recently published survey, out of 3,360 patients surveyed at a randomly chosen hospital, 75.1 percent of the patients said they are "very satisfied" with the hospital service; 20.5 percent said they are "okay" with the service. Only 4.4 percent of the patients said they are either "not satisfied" or "very not satisfied" with the service or care provided.[^cite_note-537]

The Taiwanese disease control authority is the [Taiwan Centers for Disease Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Centers_for_Disease_Control) (CDC). During the [SARS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome) outbreak in March 2003 there were 347 confirmed cases. During the outbreak the CDC and local governments set up monitoring stations throughout public transportation, recreational sites and other public areas. With full containment in July 2003, there has not been a case of SARS since.[^cite_note-538] Owing to the lessons from SARS, a [National Health Command Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Command_Center?action=edit\&redlink=1) was established in 2004, which includes the [Central Epidemic Command Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Epidemic_Command_Center) (CECC). The CECC has played a central role in Taiwan's approach to epidemics, including [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Taiwan).

In 2019, the infant mortality rate was 4.2 deaths per 1,000 live births, with 20 physicians and 71 hospital beds per 10,000 people.[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540] Life expectancy at birth in 2020 is 77.5 years and 83.9 years for males and females, respectively.[^cite_note-541]

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Taiwan), [Cultural history of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_history_of_Taiwan), and [Chinese Cultural Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Cultural_Renaissance)

See also: [Taiwanese wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_wave)



[Amis people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amis_people) of Taiwan performing a traditional dance

The cultures of Taiwan are a hybrid blend from various sources, incorporating elements of the majority [traditional Chinese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture), aboriginal cultures, [Japanese cultural influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Japan), traditional Confucianist beliefs, and increasingly, [Western values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).

During the [martial law period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Taiwan), the [Kuomintang](16903.md) promoted an official [traditional Chinese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture) over Taiwan in order to emphasize that the Republic of China represents the true orthodoxy to [Chinese Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Culture) as opposed to [Communist China](5405.md).[^cite_note-542] The government launched what's known as the [Chinese Cultural Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Cultural_Renaissance) movement in Taiwan in opposition to the cultural destructions caused by the [Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_China) during the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution). The General Assembly of Chinese Culture was established to help promote Chinese culture in Taiwan and overseas. It was Kuomintang's first structured plan for cultural development on Taiwan. The Chinese Cultural Renaissance movement in Taiwan had led to some aspects of Chinese Culture being better preserved there than in [mainland China](55079.md), for example the continued use of [Traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese). The influence of [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism) can be found in the behavior of [Taiwanese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_people), known for their friendliness and politeness.[^cite_note-543]



The [National Palace Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palace_Museum) is an institute dedicated to the organization, care, and display of ancient Chinese artifacts and works of art.

The lifting of [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Taiwan) ushered a period of [democratization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratization) whereby [Freedom of Speech and Expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_Speech) led to a flourishing [Taiwanese literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_literature) and [mass media in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Taiwan). The Taiwanese Constitution protects "speech, teaching, writing and publication."[^cite_note-544] In 2022, the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md)'s [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index) ranked Taiwan as having the second highest [democracy score in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Asia) and Australasia.[^cite_note-545] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) has ranked Taiwan the second freest place in Asia[^cite_note-546][^cite_note-547] while [CIVICUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civicus) rated Taiwan and New Zealand as the only "open" countries in the Asia-Pacific.[^cite_note-548][^cite_note-549] In the aftermath of China gaining control of Hong Kong and restricting freedom of speech and protest, 36,789 Hong Kong residents moved to Taiwan from 2019 to 2022, an average of about 9,000 immigrants per year. In 2018, Taiwan only had 4,000 Hong Kong immigrants.[^cite_note-550]

Reflecting the continuing controversy surrounding the political status of Taiwan, politics continues to play a role in the conception and development of a Taiwanese cultural identity, especially in its relationship to Chinese culture.[^cite_note-551] In recent years, the concept of Taiwanese [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) has been proposed as a relatively apolitical alternative view, which has allowed for the inclusion of mainlanders and other minority groups into the continuing re-definition of Taiwanese culture as collectively held systems of meaning and customary patterns of thought and behavior shared by the people of Taiwan.[^cite_note-552] [Identity politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_politics), along with the over one hundred years of political separation from mainland China, has led to distinct traditions in many areas, including [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_cuisine) and [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Taiwan).

### Arts

Main articles: [Music of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Taiwan) and [Taiwanese art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_art)

Teresa Teng smiling

Jay Chou performing

[Teresa Teng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Teng) (*left*) is widely recognized as a cultural icon for her contributions to Chinese pop, and [Jay Chou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Chou) (*right*) has been a leading figure in the [Mandopop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandopop) industry since the early 2000s.

Acclaimed classical musicians include violinist [Cho-Liang Lin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cho-Liang_Lin), pianist [Ching-Yun Hu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ching-Yun_Hu), and the Lincoln Center Chamber Music Society artist director [Wu Han](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wu_Han_\(pianist\)). Other musicians include [Teresa Teng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_Teng), [Jay Chou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Chou) and groups such as [Mayday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayday_\(Taiwanese_band\)) and [heavy metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_metal_music) band [Chthonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chthonic_\(band\)), led by singer [Freddy Lim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddy_Lim), which has been referred to as the "[Black Sabbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sabbath) of Asia".[^cite_note-553][^cite_note-554]

[Taiwanese films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Taiwan) have won various international awards at film festivals around the world. [Ang Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ang_Lee), a Taiwanese director, has directed critically acclaimed films such as: *[Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crouching_Tiger,_Hidden_Dragon)*; *[Eat Drink Man Woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eat_Drink_Man_Woman)*; *[Sense and Sensibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense_and_Sensibility_\(1995_film\))*; *[Brokeback Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brokeback_Mountain)*; *[Life of Pi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_of_Pi_\(film\))*; and *[Lust, Caution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lust,_Caution)*. Other famous Taiwanese directors include [Tsai Ming-liang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ming-liang), [Edward Yang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Yang), and [Hou Hsiao-hsien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hou_Hsiao-hsien). Taiwan has hosted the [Golden Horse Film Festival and Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horse_Film_Festival_and_Awards) since 1962.

The [National Palace Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palace_Museum) houses more than 650,000 pieces of Chinese bronze, jade, calligraphy, painting, and porcelain and is considered one of the greatest collections of Chinese art and objects in the world.[^cite_note-555]

### Popular culture

[Karaoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaoke) is extremely popular in Taiwan, where it is known as KTV.[^cite_note-556] KTV businesses operate in a hotel-like style, renting out small rooms and ballrooms according to the number of guests. Many KTV establishments partner with restaurants and buffets to form all-encompassing and elaborate evening affairs. Tour busses that travel around Taiwan have several TVs, primarily for singing karaoke.

Taiwan has a high density of 24-hour convenience stores, which provide services on behalf of financial institutions or government agencies, such as collection of parking fees, utility bills, traffic fines, and credit card payments.[^cite_note-557] Chains such as [FamilyMart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FamilyMart) provide clothing laundry services in select stores,[^cite_note-558] and tickets for [TRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Railways_Administration) and THSR are available at [7-Eleven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7-Eleven), FamilyMart, [Hi-Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hi-Life_\(convenience_store\)) and [OK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OK_\(convenience_store\)?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-559][^cite_note-560]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Taiwanese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_cuisine)

Taiwanese culinary history is murky and is intricately tied to patterns of migration and colonization. Local and international Taiwanese cuisine, including its history, is a politically contentious topic. Famous Taiwanese dishes include [Taiwanese beef noodle soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_beef_noodle_soup), [gua bao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gua_bao), [zongzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zongzi), [braised pork rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braised_pork_rice), [Taiwanese fried chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_fried_chicken), [oyster vermicelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyster_vermicelli), [sanbeiji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanbeiji), and [aiyu jelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aiyu_jelly).[^cite_note-Eater_2019-561] [Bubble tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubble_tea), created in Taiwan in the 1980s, has now become popular globally.[^cite_note-562] In 2014, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* called [Taiwanese night markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_night_markets) the "best street food markets in the world".[^cite_note-Guardian_2014-563] The [Michelin Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide) began reviewing restaurants in Taiwan in 2018.[^cite_note-564]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Taiwan)

See also: [List of sporting events in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sporting_events_in_Taiwan)



The [Chinese Professional Baseball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Professional_Baseball_League) (CPBL) is the top-tier professional baseball league in Taiwan.

[Baseball](3850.md) is commonly considered Taiwan's [national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport) and is a popular spectator sport.[^cite_note-565] The men's team won medals across all levels of baseball in 2022, including the [U-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_U-12_Baseball_World_Cup), [U-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_U-15_Baseball_World_Cup), [U-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_U-18_Baseball_World_Cup), [U-23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_U-23_Baseball_World_Cup), and [Baseball5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Baseball5_World_Cup) competitions, the only team to do so in baseball history.[^cite_note-566] The results made [Taiwan's national baseball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Taipei_national_baseball_team) one of the top-ranked teams in the [WBSC World Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WBSC_World_Rankings). [Professional baseball in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_baseball_in_Taiwan) started with the founding of the [Chinese Professional Baseball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Professional_Baseball_League) (CPBL) in 1989.[^cite_note-567] As of 2025, the CPBL has six teams, with an average attendance of over 10,000 per game.[^cite_note-568][^cite_note-569] Some elite players have signed with overseas professional teams in the [Major League Baseball](38776.md) (MLB) or the [Nippon Professional Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Professional_Baseball) (NPB). There have been eighteen [Taiwanese MLB players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_players_from_Taiwan) as of the 2025 MLB season, including former pitchers [Chien-Ming Wang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chien-Ming_Wang) and [Wei-Yin Chen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wei-Yin_Chen).[^cite_note-570] As for variations of baseball, Taiwan also has a strong women's [softball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Softball) team. [Its women's national softball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Taipei_women's_national_softball_team) won a bronze medal at the [2022 World Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Softball_at_the_2022_World_Games).[^cite_note-571]

[Basketball](3921.md) is Taiwan's other major sport.[^cite_note-572] The [P. League+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._League+) and [Taiwan Professional Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Professional_Basketball_League) are the country's two professional basketball leagues.[^cite_note-573] The [semi-professional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-professional_sports) [Super Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Basketball_League) (SBL) is also in play.[^cite_note-574] Other team sports include [volleyball](32558.md) and [football](10568.md). Taiwan is also a major competitor in [korfball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korfball).[^cite_note-575]

[Taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo) was introduced to Taiwan for military training and has become a mature and successful [combat sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_sport) in Taiwan.[^cite_note-576] Taekwondo athletes [Chen Shih-hsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Shih-hsin) and [Chu Mu-yen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chu_Mu-yen) were the first two Taiwanese Olympians to win gold medals on the official record at the [2004 Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo_at_the_2004_Summer_Olympics). There are many outstanding Taiwanese players in other individual sports, such as [badminton](3956.md), [tennis](29773.md), [table tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_tennis), and [golf](19568112.md). Taiwan's strength in badminton is demonstrated by [Tai Tzu-ying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_Tzu-ying), who spent the most weeks as the world No. 1 women's singles player in the [BWF World Ranking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BWF_World_Ranking), and her compatriots in the [BWF World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BWF_World_Tour).[^cite_note-577][^cite_note-578] Taiwan also has a long history of strong international presence in table tennis. Six-time Olympian [Chuang Chih-yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuang_Chih-yuan) made the most appearances at the Olympic Games among Taiwanese athletes.[^cite_note-579] [Yani Tseng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yani_Tseng) is the youngest golf player ever, male or female, to win five [major championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_major_golf_championships) and was ranked number 1 in the [Women's World Golf Rankings](4197326.md) for 109 consecutive weeks from 2011 to 2013.[^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581][^cite_note-582] In tennis, [Hsieh Su-wei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hsieh_Su-wei) is the country's most successful female player.[^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584]

Taiwan participates in international sporting organizations and events under the name "Chinese Taipei." Taiwan has hosted several [multi-sport events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-sport_event) in the past, including the [2009 World Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_World_Games) in Kaohsiung, as well as the [2009 Summer Deaflympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Summer_Deaflympics) and [2017 Summer Universiade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Summer_Universiade) in Taipei.[^cite_note-585] Hualien will host the 2026 [International Children's Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Children's_Games).[^cite_note-586] Other major recurring events held in Taiwan include:

- [Taipei Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taipei_Marathon) (marathon)

- [New Taipei City Wan Jin Shi Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Taipei_City_Wan_Jin_Shi_Marathon) (marathon)

### National symbols

Main article: [National symbols of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Taiwan)

The Seal of the Republic of China (physical object)

The seal imprint of the Seal

[The Seal of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_seals_of_the_Republic_of_China)

Official national symbols include the [flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_Republic_of_China), [anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anthem_of_the_Republic_of_China), [flower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_flower_of_Taiwan), and [seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_seals_of_the_Republic_of_China).[^cite_note-587]

### Calendar

Main article: [Republic of China calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_calendar)

See also: [Chinese calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_calendar) and [Public holidays in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Taiwan)

The standard [Gregorian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_calendar) is used for most purposes. The year is often denoted by the Minguo [era system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_era) which starts in 1912, the year the ROC was founded. 2026 is year 115 Minguo (民國115年). The [East Asian date format](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_date#Gregorian,_year%E2%80%93month%E2%80%93day_\(YMD\)) is used in Chinese.[^cite_note-588] Prior to standardization in 1929, the [Chinese calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_calendar) was officially used. It is a [Lunisolar calendar system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunisolar_calendar) which remains in use for traditional festivals such as the [Lunar New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_New_Year_in_Taiwan?action=edit\&redlink=1), the [Lantern Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lantern_Festival), and the [Dragon Boat Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_Boat_Festival).[^cite_note-589]
