[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;[Taipei](57648.md) is the official seat of [government of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Republic_of_China) although the [Constitution of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_China) does not specify the *de jure* capital.<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-4)</sup>

[^cite_note-9]: &#x20;[Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Mandarin) as the standard [variety of Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_of_Chinese)<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-推動雙語國家政策問題研析-7)</sup>\
    [Vernacular Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular_Chinese) (used in most occasions) \
    [Classical Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Chinese) (used in formal or ceremonial occasions, religious or cultural rites, official documents, legal and court rulings and judiciary documents)<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-8)</sup>

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;A national language in Taiwan is legally defined as "a natural language used by an original people group of Taiwan and the [Taiwan Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Sign_Language)".<sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-natLangAct-14)</sup>

[^cite_note-nat-lang-16]: &#x20;Not designated but meets legal definition.

[^cite_note-var-hok-17]: &#x20;Colloquially known as "Taiwanese", it is considered a variety of [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien).

[^cite_note-21]: &#x20;Mixed indigenous-Han ancestry is included in the figure for Han.

[^cite_note-26]: &#x20;While the [tricameral parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tricameralism) continues to exist *de jure*, the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Republic_of_China\)) (electoral college) was *de facto* suspended in 2005 and the [Control Yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_Yuan) (upper house) ceased to be a parliamentary chamber *de facto* in 1993 leaving the Legislative Yuan (lower house) as the *de facto* unicameral chamber.

[^cite_note-taiwan-34]: &#x20;The [HDI annual report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Report) compiled by the [UNDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDP) does not include Taiwan because it is no longer a UN member state, and is neither included as part of the People's Republic of China by both of the UNDP and SHDI reports when calculating data for China.<sup>[\[23\]](#cite_note-human_development_report-32)</sup> [Taiwan's Statistical Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate_General_of_Budget,_Accounting_and_Statistics) calculated its HDI for 2023 to be 0.934 based on UNDP's 2014 methodology, which would place Taiwan at 21st globally in 2023 within the 2025 UNDP report.<sup>[\[24\]](#cite_note-33)</sup>

[^cite_note-ROC_territory-40]: &#x20;There are four contemporary geopolitical definitions of the extent of "Taiwan": The common name referring to the state, also known as the "Republic of China" (ROC), including [all 168 islands administered by the ROC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Taiwan), collectively known as [Taiwan Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Area);<sup>[\[26\]](#cite_note-37)</sup>The traditional Taiwan region (本島地區), which consists of the main island of Taiwan and its surrounding islands, including the [Penghu islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penghu_islands),<sup>[\[27\]](#cite_note-38)</sup><sup>[\[28\]](#cite_note-39)</sup> but excludes [Kinmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinmen), [Matsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matsu_Islands), and [Wuqiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuqiu,_Kinmen), those are traditionally parts of [Fujian Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuchien_Province,_Republic_of_China), and also excluding the ROC-controlled [South China Sea Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Sea_Islands);The traditional region without Penghu, which is sometimes regarded as a separate region;The main island of Taiwan (Formosa) alone, excluding all the offshore islands nearby.

[^cite_note-44]: &#x20;Bellwood's definition: "Island Southeast Asia includes Taiwan, the Philippines, Brunei and the Sarawak and Sabah provinces of East Malaysia (northern Borneo), and all of the islands of Indonesia to the west of New Guinea."

[^cite_note-45]: &#x20;Robert Blust: "The major western island groups include the great Indonesian, or Malay Archipelago, to its north the smaller and more compact Philippine Archipelago, and still further north at 22 to 25 degrees north latitude and some 150 kilometres from the coast of China, the island of Taiwan (Formosa). Together these island groups constitute insular (or island) Southeast Asia."

[^cite_note-Southeast_Asia-46]: &#x20;Despite the conventional definition to include Taiwan as part of East Asia, there is some variability as to whether Taiwan is also included in the region of [Maritime Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Southeast_Asia). Some scholars, such as [Peter Bellwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Bellwood) and [Robert Blust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Blust),<sup>[\[30\]](#cite_note-Bellwood_2017-43)</sup><sup>[\[l\]](#cite_note-44)</sup><sup>[\[m\]](#cite_note-45)</sup> include Taiwan as part of [Southeast Asia](28741.md) in their definition.

[^cite_note-Taiwan_Islands-57]: &#x20;According to official data from [Executive Yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Yuan) and local governments of Taiwan, [Taiwan Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Area) consists of total 168 naturally occurring islands. Taiwan (Formosa) and its offshore islands (22)<sup>[\[31\]](#cite_note-TWN-gov-47)</sup>[Penghu Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penghu_Islands) (90)<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-Penghu1-48)</sup><sup>[\[33\]](#cite_note-Penghu2-49)</sup>[Kinmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinmen), including [Wuqiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuqiu,_Kinmen) (17)<sup>[\[34\]](#cite_note-Kinmen1-50)</sup><sup>[\[35\]](#cite_note-Kinmen2-51)</sup><sup>[\[36\]](#cite_note-Kinmen3-52)</sup>[Matsu Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matsu_Islands) (36)<sup>[\[37\]](#cite_note-Matsu1-53)</sup><sup>[\[38\]](#cite_note-Matsu2-54)</sup>[Pratas Island](410487.md) (1)<sup>[\[39\]](#cite_note-lungtsunni-55)</sup><sup>[\[40\]](#cite_note-USStateDept-56)</sup>[Spratly Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spratly_Islands) (2, [Taiping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiping_Island) and [Zhongzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhongzhou_Reef)) Note: The [Senkaku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senkaku_Islands) (known as "Diaoyu" or "Diaoyutai" in standard Chinese), which are an archipelago of 8 islands and islets in the East China Sea, are controlled by Japan, and are [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senkaku_Islands_dispute) by the ROC and the PRC (People's Republic of China) as being a part of Taiwan. Japan administers the Senkaku Islands as a part of the [Ryukyu Islands](8458939.md).

[^cite_note-HDI-1-68]: &#x20;The [HDI annual report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Report) compiled by the [UNDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDP) does not include Taiwan because it is no longer a UN member state, and is neither included as part of the People's Republic of China by the UNDP when calculating data for China.<sup>[\[23\]](#cite_note-human_development_report-32)</sup> [Taiwan's Statistical Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate_General_of_Budget,_Accounting_and_Statistics) calculated its HDI for 2021 to be 0.926 based on UNDP's 2010 methodology,<sup>[\[47\]](#cite_note-64)</sup><sup>[\[48\]](#cite_note-65)</sup> which would place Taiwan at 19th globally in 2021 within the 2022 UNDP report.<sup>[\[49\]](#cite_note-HDI_2021-66)</sup><sup>[\[50\]](#cite_note-67)</sup>

[^cite_note-88]: &#x20;Although this is the present meaning of *guó*, in [Old Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Chinese) (when its pronunciation was something like /\*qʷˤək/)<sup>[\[68\]](#cite_note-bs-86)</sup> it meant the walled city of the Chinese and the areas they could control from them.<sup>[\[69\]](#cite_note-wilx-87)</sup>

[^cite_note-90]: &#x20;Its use is attested from the sixth-century [Classic of History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classic_of_History), which states "[Huangtian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tian_\(god\)) bestowed the lands and the peoples of the central state to the ancestors" (皇天既付中國民越厥疆土于先王).<sup>[\[70\]](#cite_note-89)</sup>

[^cite_note-214]: &#x20;Interpretations on whether sovereignty was transferred to the ROC varies. ROC took control of Taiwan under [General Order No. 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Order_No._1), on behalf of the [Allies of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II). Taiwan was simultaneously established as a [ROC province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_\(Republic_of_China_province\)), though opinions differed among the Allies on the unilateral announcement of annexation of Taiwan by the ROC. Japan later renounced its claims to Taiwan and the Pescadores in the [Treaty of San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_San_Francisco) in 1952; see [Retrocession Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrocession_Day), [Theory of the Undetermined Status of Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_the_Undetermined_Status_of_Taiwan), [political status of Taiwan](66555.md) and [1943 Cairo Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1943_Cairo_Declaration).

[^cite_note-324]: &#x20;Also known as the Taiwan area or Tai–Min area ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 臺閩地區; lit. 'Taiwan–Fujian area')

[^cite_note-325]: &#x20;The mainland area consists of Mainland China, [Tibet](31516.md) and (previously) [Outer Mongolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Mongolia)

[^cite_note-328]: &#x20;Nominal; provincial governments have been abolished

[^cite_note-329]: &#x20;Constitutionally having the same structure as the free area, these are currently under the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) control with a different structure

[^cite_note-Mair-81]: &#x20;[Mair, Victor H.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_H._Mair) (2003). ["How to Forget Your Mother Tongue and Remember Your National Language"](http://pinyin.info/readings/mair/taiwanese.html). *Pīnyīn.info*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20141213183153/http://pinyin.info/readings/mair/taiwanese.html) from the original on 13 December 2014. Retrieved 5 April 2018. The true derivation of the name "Taiwan" is actually from the ethnonym of a tribe in the southwest part of the island in the area around Ping'an. As early as 1636, a Dutch missionary referred to this group as Taiouwang. From the name of the tribe, the Portuguese called the area around Ping'an as Tayowan, Taiyowan, Tyovon, Teijoan, Toyouan, and so forth. Indeed, already in his ship's log of 1622, the Dutchman Cornelis Reijersen referred to the area as Teijoan and Taiyowan.

[^cite_note-482]: &#x20;Lo, Yun-Hua (2021). ["Detecting Genetic Ancestry and Adaptation in the Taiwanese Han People"](https://academic.oup.com/mbe/article/38/10/4149/5955855). *[Molecular Biology and Evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_Biology_and_Evolution)*. **38** (10): 4149–4165. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/molbev/msaa276](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Fmolbev%2Fmsaa276). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [8476137](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8476137). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [33170928](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33170928). Retrieved 18 October 2022. Consequently, our results showed that the ISEA ancestry in the Taiwanese Han was the outcome of population mixtures rather than shared ancestry, and the admixture event likely occurred before the Taiwanese Han ancestors migrated to Taiwan (fig. 2A). If the admixture occurred only after the Han people migrated to Taiwan, then the observed results would only be seen in the Taiwanese Han. However, similar F3 outcomes were found in the Chinese Han (supplementary fig. S2, Supplementary Material online), supporting that admixture occurred prior to migration to Taiwan. Moreover, signatures of population admixture were also detected between the ancestors of Taiwanese Han and the Ami Austronesian-speaking population using the F4 test; significant positive F4 values were observed when most Sino-Tibetan speaking populations were individually included in the analysis, except for the Chinese Singapore and Chinese Cantonese (table 1). These two populations appear to be genetically closest to the Taiwanese Han among all other Sino-Tibetan speaking populations (fig. 1B), which is consistent with the hypothesis of population mixture before the ancestors of Taiwanese Han migrated to Taiwan.
